It has been consistently present every single day, like a non-stop roller coaster of panic and dread where I'm constantly replaying those conversations in my head.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are any specific thoughts or memories related to the potential separation that you find yourself persistently preoccupied with, or if it's more of a general feeling of anxiety and dread about the future?"

I've also been really irritable, snapping at the kids over little things, and then lying awake half the night replaying every conversation in my head.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me how long you've been experiencing these intense emotional swings and functional difficulties since your wife first mentioned separating?\n\nPatient: It’s been going on for about a month now, starting almost immediately after my wife first brought up the separation. All of these difficulties really ramped up within just a few days of that conversation.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant stressors or traumatic events besides the potential separation from your wife in the last few months, or has this been the primary source of distress for you?\n\nPatient: The separation is absolutely the primary source of my distress, and everything spiraled right after that conversation. It did happen to coincide with a highly stressful time at work where I was finalizing a major contract for a marquee client, which made juggling my focus even harder. But the crisis with my wife is what really threw me off balance.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if there are any moments or periods where you feel completely disconnected from the emotional pain of the potential separation, or if the distress has been consistently present since the conversation with your wife?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any periods where I feel disconnected or numb to the pain. It has been consistently present every single day, like a non-stop roller coaster of panic and dread where I'm constantly replaying those conversations in my head.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are any specific thoughts or memories related to the potential separation that you find yourself persistently preoccupied with, or if it's more of a general feeling of anxiety and dread about the future?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. I constantly find myself replaying our conversations in my head and second-guessing every interaction at home, panicking that I’ve blown up my family. But there's also this constant dread about the future, like when I'm sitting in my car in the driveway, dreading going inside and wondering how I'm going to keep my life and work from falling apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself persistently preoccupied with thoughts of self-blame, worthlessness, or guilt specifically related to the potential separation, or is your preoccupation more focused on the practical and emotional consequences of the separation for you and your family?\n\nPatient: It's honestly a mix of both. I feel a deep sense of guilt and constantly blame myself, replaying past conversations and worrying that I've completely blown up my family. At the same time, I'm highly preoccupied with the practical fallout, like how this is affecting my kids and my inability to focus on closing deals at work when my structured home life is falling apart."

But the crisis with my wife is what really threw me off balance.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if there are any moments or periods where you feel completely disconnected from the emotional pain of the potential separation, or if the distress has been consistently present since the conversation with your wife?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any periods where I feel disconnected or numb to the pain. It has been consistently present every single day, like a non-stop roller coaster of panic and dread where I'm constantly replaying those conversations in my head.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are any specific thoughts or memories related to the potential separation that you find yourself persistently preoccupied with, or if it's more of a general feeling of anxiety and dread about the future?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. I constantly find myself replaying our conversations in my head and second-guessing every interaction at home, panicking that I’ve blown up my family. But there's also this constant dread about the future, like when I'm sitting in my car in the driveway, dreading going inside and wondering how I'm going to keep my life and work from falling apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself persistently preoccupied with thoughts of self-blame, worthlessness, or guilt specifically related to the potential separation, or is your preoccupation more focused on the practical and emotional consequences of the separation for you and your family?\n\nPatient: It's honestly a mix of both. I feel a deep sense of guilt and constantly blame myself, replaying past conversations and worrying that I've completely blown up my family. At the same time, I'm highly preoccupied with the practical fallout, like how this is affecting my kids and my inability to focus on closing deals at work when my structured home life is falling apart.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if the guilt and self-blame you're experiencing are primarily focused on perceived failures in your relationship or if they extend to other areas of your life, such as your role as a parent or your performance at work?\n\nPatient: While it started with feeling like I blew up my marriage, the guilt has definitely extended to other areas of my life. I feel terrible about being so distracted and snapping at my kids, which has left my wife to handle all of their logistics because I've been too unreliable. It has also bled into my work, making me worry about losing my professional focus and stalling major deals because I'm missing key details.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt as though you were outside observers of your life, or like you were in a dream, since your wife mentioned separating?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like that at all. I don't feel like an outside observer or like I'm in a dream; in fact, everything feels painfully real and overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any experiences where you felt detached from yourself or your surroundings, or like you were 'going through the motions' without being fully present, since the separation crisis began?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I haven't really experienced feeling detached from myself or my surroundings. While I get highly distracted, unfocused, and sometimes zone out in my car because I'm dreading going inside, I don't feel disconnected from reality or my own body.

I realized I needed to talk to someone because I'm just not handling this crisis the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how you've been feeling emotionally since your wife mentioned separating, such as feelings of sadness, anxiety, or detachment from others?\n\nPatient: It’s been a complete emotional roller coaster where one minute I’m panicking that I’ve ruined my family, and the next I'm just sitting paralyzed in my car in the driveway, dreading going inside. I've also been really irritable, snapping at the kids over little things, and then lying awake half the night replaying every conversation in my head.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me how long you've been experiencing these intense emotional swings and functional difficulties since your wife first mentioned separating?\n\nPatient: It’s been going on for about a month now, starting almost immediately after my wife first brought up the separation. All of these difficulties really ramped up within just a few days of that conversation.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant stressors or traumatic events besides the potential separation from your wife in the last few months, or has this been the primary source of distress for you?\n\nPatient: The separation is absolutely the primary source of my distress, and everything spiraled right after that conversation. It did happen to coincide with a highly stressful time at work where I was finalizing a major contract for a marquee client, which made juggling my focus even harder. But the crisis with my wife is what really threw me off balance.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if there are any moments or periods where you feel completely disconnected from the emotional pain of the potential separation, or if the distress has been consistently present since the conversation with your wife?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any periods where I feel disconnected or numb to the pain. It has been consistently present every single day, like a non-stop roller coaster of panic and dread where I'm constantly replaying those conversations in my head.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are any specific thoughts or memories related to the potential separation that you find yourself persistently preoccupied with, or if it's more of a general feeling of anxiety and dread about the future?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. I constantly find myself replaying our conversations in my head and second-guessing every interaction at home, panicking that I’ve blown up my family. I feel a deep sense of guilt and constantly blame myself, replaying past conversations and worrying that I've completely blown up my family. I feel terrible about being so distracted and snapping at my kids, which has left my wife to handle all of their logistics because I've been too unreliable. It has also bled into my work, making me worry about losing my professional focus and stalling major deals because I'm missing key details.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt as though you were outside observers of your life, or like you were in a dream, since your wife mentioned separating?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like that at all. I don't feel like an outside observer or like I'm in a dream; in fact, everything feels painfully real and overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any experiences where you felt detached from yourself or your surroundings, or like you were 'going through the motions' without being fully present, since the separation crisis began?\n\nPatient: I haven't really experienced feeling detached from myself or my surroundings. While I get highly distracted, unfocused, and sometimes zone out in my car because I'm dreading going inside, I don't feel disconnected from reality or my own body.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you haven't felt detached from yourself or your surroundings, but can you clarify if there have been moments where things around you seemed unreal or distant, even if you felt connected to your own body and emotions?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any moments where my surroundings or the things around me seemed unreal or distant. Everything feels very real, almost painfully so, and I'm acutely aware of what is happening."

I don't feel like an outside observer or like I'm in a dream; in fact, everything feels painfully real and overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any experiences where you felt detached from yourself or your surroundings, or like you were 'going through the motions' without being fully present, since the separation crisis began?\n\nPatient: I haven't really experienced feeling detached from myself or my surroundings. Everything feels very real, almost painfully so, and I'm acutely aware of what is happening.\n\nDoctor: To further clarify, are you certain you haven't experienced any brief moments, even if very fleeting, where either you felt like you were observing yourself from outside your body or the world around you seemed foggy or far away, since your wife mentioned the separation?\n\nPatient: No, I am certain I haven't experienced anything like that. I don't feel like an outside observer or like I'm in a dream; in fact, everything feels painfully real and overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any experiences where you felt detached from yourself or your surroundings, or like you were 'going through the motions' without being fully present, since the separation crisis began?\n\nPatient: I haven't really experienced feeling detached from myself or my surroundings. I haven't experienced anything like feeling in a dream or having things seem unreal, even for a brief moment, since my wife brought up separating. I feel a deep sense of guilt and constantly blame myself, replaying past conversations and worrying that I've completely blown up my family. At the same time, I'm highly preoccupied with the practical fallout, like how this is affecting my kids and my inability to focus on closing deals at work when my structured home life is falling apart.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if the guilt and self-blame you're experiencing are primarily focused on perceived failures in your relationship or if they extend to other areas of your life, such as your role as a parent or your performance at work?\n\nPatient: While it started with feeling like I blew up my marriage, the guilt has definitely extended to other areas of my life. I feel terrible about being so distracted and snapping at my kids, which has left my wife to handle all of their logistics because I've been too unreliable. It has also bled into my work, making me worry about losing my professional focus and stalling major deals because I'm missing key details.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt as though you were outside observers of your life, or like you were in a dream, since your wife mentioned separating?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like that at all. I don't feel like an outside observer or like I'm in a dream; in fact, everything feels painfully real and overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any experiences where you felt detached from yourself or your surroundings, or like you were 'going through the motions' without being fully present, since the separation crisis began?\n\nPatient: I haven't really experienced feeling detached from myself or my surroundings. While I get highly distracted, unfocused, and sometimes zone out in my car because I'm dreading going inside, I don't feel disconnected from reality or my own body.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you haven't felt detached from yourself or your surroundings, but can you clarify if there have been moments where things around you seemed unreal or distant, even if you felt connected to your own body and emotions?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any moments where my surroundings or the things around me seemed unreal or distant. Everything feels very real, almost painfully so, and I'm acutely aware of what is happening.\n\nDoctor: To further clarify, are you certain you haven't experienced any brief moments, even if very fleeting, where either you felt like you were observing yourself from outside your body or the world around you seemed foggy or far away, since your wife mentioned the separation?\n\nPatient: No, I am certain I haven't experienced anything like that. I have never felt like I was observing myself from outside my body, and the world around me has never seemed foggy or far away.\n\nDoctor: Are you absolutely sure you've never had even a brief moment where you felt like you were in a dream or things seemed unreal, even if it was just for a second, since your wife brought up separating?\n\nPatient: Yes, I am completely sure. Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.
